## Lesson 49: Properties of: regular polygons and parallelograms

Prerequisites: 48 · Skill: m2-49 (Properties of regular polygons and parallelograms)

**Note** (draft)

> Parallelogram: opposite sides are parallel and equal; opposite angles are equal; two angles next to each other add up to 180°; the diagonals cut each other in half (they bisect each other).
> Special parallelograms: rectangle (four right angles, equal diagonals), rhombus (four equal sides, diagonals perpendicular), square (both).
> Regular polygon with n sides: each exterior angle = 360° ÷ n; each interior angle = 180° − exterior angle; it has n lines of symmetry.

**Card c49-1** (draft)

> In a parallelogram, two angles next to each other add up to 180°.
>
> _70° and 110°_  

Check question: `m2-49-adj` · number, level 1 · computed by rule, reused from practice

> In parallelogram ABCD, Â = 84°. Find B̂.

Answer: **96 °**
- Typed **84** (equal) → “Â and B̂ are next to each other: they add up to 180°. Only opposite angles are equal.”

Full working after a second miss:

> Â and B̂ are next to each other.  
> 180 − 84 = 96°.  

**Card c49-2** (draft)

> Opposite angles of a parallelogram are equal; opposite sides too.
>
> _Â = Ĉ, B̂ = D̂, AB = DC_  

Check question: `m2-49-opp` · number, level 1 · computed by rule, reused from practice

> In parallelogram ABCD, B̂ = 94°. Find D̂.

Answer: **94 °**
- Typed **86** (supp) → “B̂ and D̂ are opposite, not next to each other: they are equal.”

Full working after a second miss:

> B̂ and D̂ are opposite angles.  
> Opposite angles are equal: D̂ = 94°.  

**Card c49-3** (draft)

> The diagonals of a parallelogram cut each other in half.
>
> _AC = 14 cm, so AO = 7 cm_  

Check question: `m2-49-diag-card` · number, level 1 · computed by rule

> The diagonals of parallelogram ABCD meet at O. AC = 10 cm. Find AO.

Answer: **5 cm**
- Typed **10** (whole) → “O is the midpoint: AO is half of AC.”

Full working after a second miss:

> The diagonals bisect each other: O is the midpoint of [AC].  
> AO = 10 ÷ 2 = 5 cm.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> In parallelogram ABCD, Â = 65°. Find B̂, Ĉ and D̂.

1. Next to each other: B̂ = 180 − 65 = 115°.
2. Opposite angles are equal: Ĉ = 65°, D̂ = 115°.

**Answer:** B̂ = 115°, Ĉ = 65°, D̂ = 115°

## Lesson 50: Area of a parallelogram and of a trapezium

Prerequisites: 49, 32 · Skill: m2-50 (The area of a parallelogram and of a trapezium)

**Note** (draft)

> Area of a parallelogram = base × height (the height is perpendicular to the base, not the slanting side).
> Area of a trapezium = ½ × (sum of the parallel sides) × height = ½ (a + b) h.
> Example: a trapezium with parallel sides 9 cm and 5 cm and height 4 cm: ½ × (9 + 5) × 4 = 28 cm².

**Card c50-1** (draft)

> Parallelogram: base × height.
>
> _8 × 5 = 40 cm²_

Answer: **56 cm²**
- Typed **28** (half) → “That is a triangle's formula. A parallelogram is base × height (no ½).”

Full working after a second miss:

> Area = base × height.  
> 7 × 8 = 56 cm².  

**Card c50-2** (draft)

> Trapezium: ½ × (a + b) × h.
>
> _½ × (10 + 6) × 5 = 40 cm²_

Answer: **78 cm²**

- Typed **432** (product) → “Add the parallel sides, do not multiply them.”

Full working after a second miss:

> Add the parallel sides: 18 + 8 = 26.  
> ½ × 26 × 6 = 78 cm².  

**Card c50-3** (draft)

> Use the perpendicular height, not the slanting side.
>

Answer: **11 cm**

## Lesson 51: Point and line symmetry

Prerequisites: 48 · Skill: m2-51 (Point and line symmetry)

**Note** (draft)

> A figure has line symmetry (an axis of symmetry) if folding it along the line makes the two halves match. A square has 4 lines of symmetry, a rectangle 2, an equilateral triangle 3, a regular polygon with n sides n.
> A figure has point symmetry (central symmetry) if a half turn (180°) about a point leaves it unchanged: parallelograms (and so rectangles, rhombuses, squares) and circles have it; triangles do not.
> On a grid: the image of a point by reflection in the vertical line x = k is at the same height, the same distance on the other side: x′ = 2k − x.
> By point symmetry about centre C, the image of M is M′ such that C is the midpoint of [MM′].

**Card c51-1** (draft)

> A line of symmetry folds the figure onto itself.
>
> _A rectangle has 2 lines of symmetry._  

Check question: `m2-51-lines` · number, level 1 · computed by rule, reused from practice

> How many lines of symmetry has a kite?

Answer: **1**

Full working after a second miss:

> Fold the kite in your mind along each possible line.  
> a kite has 1.  

**Card c51-2** (draft)

> Point symmetry: a half turn about a point leaves the figure unchanged.
>
> _Every parallelogram has point symmetry about the meeting point of its diagonals._  

Check question: `m2-51-point` · multiple choice, level 1 · computed by rule, reused from practice

> Has a kite point symmetry?

- ✅ No
- ◻️ Yes  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: After a half turn the kite points the other way._

Full working after a second miss:

> Turn the kite half a turn about its centre: it does not look the same.  
> So the answer is No.  

**Card c51-3** (draft)

> Reflection in x = k: the image is the same distance on the other side.
>
> _(−2, 1) → (4, 1) in x = 1_  

Check question: `m2-51-reflect-card` · number, level 1 · computed by rule

> Find the x-coordinate of the image of A(2, 3) by reflection in the line x = −1.

![drawing](figures/m2-51-reflect-card-95.svg)

Answer: **−4**
- Typed **−2** (negated) → “Reflecting in x = −1 is not just changing the sign (that is only for x = 0).”

Full working after a second miss:

> A is 3 units from the line.  
> Its image is 3 units on the other side: x′ = −4.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> Find the image of A(1, 3) by reflection in the line x = 3.

![drawing](figures/worked-51-96.svg)

1. A is 2 units to the left of the line.
2. Its image is 2 units to the right: x′ = 3 + 2 = 5; y stays 3.

**Answer:** A′(5, 3)

**Practice** `m2-51-lines` · number, level 1 · computed by rule

Answer: **4**

Full working after a second miss:

> Fold the square in your mind along each possible line.  
> a square has 4.

Answer: **1**

Full working after a second miss:

> Fold the isosceles trapezium in your mind along each possible line.  
> an isosceles trapezium has 1.  

**Practice** `m2-51-point` · multiple choice, level 1 · computed by rule

- ✅ Yes

> Turn the rhombus half a turn about its centre: it looks the same.

- ◻️ Yes

- Typed **3** (negated) → “Reflecting in x = 1 is not just changing the sign (that is only for x = 0).”

**Practice** `m2-51-half` · number, level 3 · computed by rule

> M has coordinates (−3, 2). Find the coordinates of its image M′ by point symmetry about C(−2, 1). Give the x-coordinate of M′.

![drawing](figures/m2-51-half-99.svg)

Answer: **−1**
- Typed **3** (origin) → “The centre is C, not the origin: x′ = 2 × −2 − −3.”
- Typed **−1.5** (half) → “Go the whole distance again beyond C, not half.”

Full working after a second miss:

> C is the midpoint of [MM′], so x′ = 2 × −2 − −3.  
> x′ = −1.  

> M has coordinates (−3, 0). Find the coordinates of its image M′ by point symmetry about C(0, −1). Give the x-coordinate of M′.

![drawing](figures/m2-51-half-100.svg)

Answer: **3**
- Typed **1.5** (half) → “Go the whole distance again beyond C, not half.”

Full working after a second miss:

> C is the midpoint of [MM′], so x′ = 2 × 0 − −3.  
> x′ = 3.  

## Lesson 52: Properties of conservation: Distances, angles, shapes, parallelism, orthogonally and points on a line

Prerequisites: 51 · Skill: m2-52 (Properties kept by symmetry)

**Note** (draft)

> A reflection (line symmetry) and a half turn (point symmetry) do not change the size or shape of a figure. They keep (conserve):
> • distances (lengths): A′B′ = AB;
> • angles: the image of a 40° angle is a 40° angle;
> • shapes: the image of a triangle is an equal (congruent) triangle;
> • parallelism: parallel lines have parallel images;
> • orthogonality: perpendicular lines have perpendicular images;
> • alignment: points on a line have images on a line.

**Card c52-1** (draft)

> Symmetry keeps lengths.
>
> _AB = 6 cm, so A′B′ = 6 cm._  

Check question: `m2-52-len` · number, level 1 · computed by rule, reused from practice

> [A′B′] is the image of [AB] by point symmetry. AB = 13 cm. Find A′B′.

Answer: **13 cm**
- Typed **26** (double) → “The image is the same size: point symmetry does not stretch.”

Full working after a second miss:

> Point symmetry keeps lengths.  
> A′B′ = 13 cm.  

**Card c52-2** (draft)

> Symmetry keeps angles.
>
> _B̂ = 55°, so B̂′ = 55°._  

Check question: `m2-52-angle` · number, level 1 · computed by rule, reused from practice

> Triangle A′B′C′ is the image of ABC by a reflection. B̂ = 68°. Find B̂′.

Answer: **68 °**
- Typed **112** (supp) → “A reflection keeps angles: the image angle is equal.”

Full working after a second miss:

> A reflection keeps angles.  
> B̂′ = 68°.  

**Card c52-3** (draft)

> Symmetry keeps parallel and perpendicular lines, and points on a line.
>
> _If (AB) ∥ (CD), then (A′B′) ∥ (C′D′)._  

Check question: `m2-52-what-card` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**

> Which statement is true?

- ◻️ The image of an angle of 30° by a half turn is an angle of 150°.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Symmetry keeps angles: it is still 30°._
- ◻️ The image of a square by a reflection can be a rectangle that is not a square.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Symmetry keeps lengths, so all four sides stay equal._
- ✅ The image of two parallel lines by a reflection is two parallel lines.

Full working after a second miss:

> The true statement is “The image of two parallel lines by a reflection is two parallel lines.”.  
> “The image of an angle of 30° by a half turn is an angle of 150°.” is false: Symmetry keeps angles: it is still 30°.  
> “The image of a square by a reflection can be a rectangle that is not a square.” is false: Symmetry keeps lengths, so all four sides stay equal.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> Triangle A′B′C′ is the image of ABC by a reflection. AB = 5 cm and Â = 40°. Find A′B′ and Â′.

1. A reflection keeps lengths: A′B′ = AB = 5 cm.
2. It keeps angles: Â′ = Â = 40°.

**Answer:** A′B′ = 5 cm, Â′ = 40°

## Lesson 53: Circumscribed circle of a triangle and of a right angled triangle

Prerequisites: 29, 31 · Skill: m2-53 (The circumscribed circle of a triangle)

**Note** (draft)

> The circumscribed circle of a triangle passes through its three vertices. Its centre O is where the three perpendicular bisectors of the sides meet (the circumcentre): O is the same distance from A, B and C.
> To draw it: draw two perpendicular bisectors; where they cross is O; the radius is OA.
> In a right-angled triangle, the centre is the midpoint of the hypotenuse, and the radius is half the hypotenuse.
> Example: a right-angled triangle with sides 6, 8, 10 cm has a circumscribed circle of radius 5 cm.

**Card c53-1** (draft)

> The circumcentre is where the perpendicular bisectors meet.
>
> _It is the same distance from the three vertices._  

Check question: `m2-53-centre` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Where is the centre of the circumscribed circle of a triangle?

- ◻️ where the heights meet  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is the orthocentre._
- ◻️ where the angle bisectors meet  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is the centre of the inscribed circle._
- ✅ where the perpendicular bisectors of the sides meet
- ◻️ where the medians meet  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is the centroid._

Full working after a second miss:

> Every point of a perpendicular bisector is the same distance from both ends of the side.  
> So the answer is “where the perpendicular bisectors of the sides meet”.  

**Card c53-2** (draft)

> Right-angled triangle: the centre is the midpoint of the hypotenuse.
>
> _Hypotenuse 13 cm → radius 6.5 cm_  

Check question: `m2-53-hyp` · number, level 1 · computed by rule, reused from practice

> A right-angled triangle has a hypotenuse of 29 cm. Find the radius of its circumscribed circle.

Answer: **14.5 cm**
- Typed **29** (diam) → “The hypotenuse is the diameter; the radius is half of it.”

Full working after a second miss:

> The hypotenuse is a diameter.  
> Radius = 29 ÷ 2 = 14.5 cm.  

**Card c53-3** (draft)

> Find the hypotenuse with Pythagoras first.
>
> _5, 12 → 13 → radius 6.5_  

Check question: `m2-53-legs-card` · number, level 1 · computed by rule

> A right-angled triangle has sides 10 cm and 24 cm next to the right angle. Find the radius of its circumscribed circle.

Answer: **13 cm**
- Typed **26** (diam) → “That is the diameter (the hypotenuse). Halve it.”
- Typed **12** (leg) → “The diameter is the hypotenuse, not a shorter side.”

Full working after a second miss:

> Hypotenuse: √(100 + 576) = 26 cm.  
> Radius = 26 ÷ 2 = 13 cm.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> A right-angled triangle has sides 6 cm and 8 cm next to the right angle. Find the radius of its circumscribed circle.

1. Hypotenuse: √(6² + 8²) = 10 cm.
2. The centre is the midpoint of the hypotenuse: radius = 10 ÷ 2 = 5 cm.

**Answer:** 5 cm

**Practice** `m2-53-centre` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**

> Where is the centre of the circumscribed circle of a right-angled triangle?

- ✅ at the midpoint of the hypotenuse
- ◻️ outside the triangle  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: For a right-angled triangle it is on the hypotenuse._
- ◻️ at the midpoint of the shortest side  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: It is the midpoint of the longest side._
- ◻️ at the right-angle vertex  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The right-angle vertex is on the circle._

Full working after a second miss:

> The hypotenuse is a diameter of the circle.  
> So the answer is “at the midpoint of the hypotenuse”.  

> Where is the centre of the circumscribed circle of a triangle?

- ◻️ where the medians meet  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is the centroid._
- ◻️ where the heights meet  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is the orthocentre._
- ◻️ where the angle bisectors meet  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is the centre of the inscribed circle._
- ✅ where the perpendicular bisectors of the sides meet

Full working after a second miss:

> Every point of a perpendicular bisector is the same distance from both ends of the side.  
> So the answer is “where the perpendicular bisectors of the sides meet”.  

**Practice** `m2-53-hyp` · number, level 1 · computed by rule

Answer: **12.5 cm**
- Typed **25** (diam) → “The hypotenuse is the diameter; the radius is half of it.”

Full working after a second miss:

> The hypotenuse is a diameter.  
> Radius = 25 ÷ 2 = 12.5 cm.

Answer: **2.5 cm**

## Lesson 54: Relative positions of two circles

Prerequisites: 53 · Skill: m2-54 (The relative positions of two circles)

**Note** (draft)

> Two circles with radii r and R (R ≥ r) and distance d between their centres can be:
> • separate (outside each other): d > R + r;
> • touching externally (one point): d = R + r;
> • intersecting in two points: R − r < d < R + r;
> • touching internally (one point, one inside the other): d = R − r;
> • one inside the other without touching: d < R − r (concentric when d = 0).
> Compare d with R + r and with R − r.

**Card c54-1** (draft)

> d = R + r: touching externally.
>
> _4 + 2 = 6 = d_  

Check question: `m2-54-touch` · number, level 1 · computed by rule, reused from practice

> Two circles with radii 10 cm and 9 cm touch externally. How far apart are their centres?

Answer: **19 cm**
- Typed **1** (inner) → “That is for touching internally. Externally, add the radii.”

Full working after a second miss:

> Touching externally: d = R + r.  
> 10 + 9 = 19 cm.  

**Card c54-2** (draft)

> d = R − r: touching internally.
>
> _6 − 2 = 4 = d_  

Check question: `m2-54-inner-card` · number, level 1 · computed by rule

> Two circles with radii 15 cm and 2 cm touch internally. How far apart are their centres?

Answer: **13 cm**
- Typed **17** (outer) → “That is for touching externally. Internally, subtract.”

Full working after a second miss:

> Touching internally: d = R − r.  
> 15 − 2 = 13 cm.  

**Card c54-3** (draft)

> Compare d with R + r and R − r to name the position.
>
> _R − r < d < R + r: intersecting._  

Check question: `m2-54-name` · multiple choice, level 1 · computed by rule, reused from practice

> Two circles have radii 8 cm and 5 cm. Their centres are 2 cm apart. What is their position?

- ◻️ intersecting in two points  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Intersecting needs 3 < d < 13._
- ◻️ separate (outside each other)  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Separate needs d > 13._
- ◻️ touching externally  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Touching externally needs d = 13 exactly._
- ✅ one inside the other

Full working after a second miss:

> R + r = 13, R − r = 3, d = 2.  
> So they are one inside the other.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> Circles with radii 5 cm and 3 cm have centres 7 cm apart. What is their position?

1. R + r = 8 and R − r = 2.
2. 2 < 7 < 8: they intersect in two points.

**Answer:** intersecting in two points

**Practice** `m2-54-name` · multiple choice, level 1 · computed by rule

> Two circles have radii 3 cm and 1 cm. Their centres are 3 cm apart. What is their position?

- ✅ intersecting in two points
- ◻️ separate (outside each other)  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Separate needs d > 4._
- ◻️ touching externally  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Touching externally needs d = 4 exactly._
- ◻️ touching internally  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Touching internally needs d = 2 exactly._

Full working after a second miss:

> R + r = 4, R − r = 2, d = 3.  
> So they are intersecting in two points.  

> Two circles have radii 7 cm and 1 cm. Their centres are 8 cm apart. What is their position?

- ◻️ touching internally  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Touching internally needs d = 6 exactly._
- ◻️ one inside the other  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: One inside the other needs d < 6._
- ✅ touching externally
- ◻️ separate (outside each other)  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Separate needs d > 8._

Full working after a second miss:

> R + r = 8, R − r = 6, d = 8.  
> So they are touching externally.

Answer: **10 cm**
- Typed **6** (inner) → “That is for touching internally. Externally, add the radii.”

Full working after a second miss:

> Touching externally: d = R + r.  
> 8 + 2 = 10 cm.

Answer: **10 cm**

> 5 + 5 = 10 cm.

Answer: **13 cm**

Full working after a second miss:

Answer: **3 cm**
- Typed **19** (outer) → “That is for touching externally. Internally, subtract.”

> 11 − 8 = 3 cm.  

**Practice** `m2-54-range` · number, level 3 · computed by rule

> Two circles have radii 5 cm and 3 cm and intersect in two points. The distance between their centres is a whole number of centimetres. What is the largest it can be?

Answer: **7 cm**

Full working after a second miss:

> To intersect: d < R + r = 8.  
> The largest whole number below 8 is 7 cm.

Full working after a second miss:

> The largest whole number below 5 is 4 cm.
